We study how LLMs can learn these interaction capabilities and transfer them to unseen domains. We propose GraphDancer, a reinforcement learning (RL) framework that teaches LLMs to navigate graphs by interleaving reasoning and function execution. To improve RL for moderate-sized LLMs, we introduce a graph-aware curriculum that schedules training by the structural difficulty of questions using an easy-to-hard biased sampler. We evaluate GraphDancer on the multi-domain GRBench benchmark, training only on Academic and Biomedical graphs. Testing covers held-out questions from these domains as well as unseen E-commerce, Literature, and Legal graphs. With either Qwen2.5-3B-Instruct or Qwen3-14B as the backbone, GraphDancer outperforms graph-agent baselines using the same backbone across all evaluation domains. These results indicate that the learned graph exploration and reasoning capabilities transfer across domains. On average across unseen domains, GraphDancer with a 14B backbone also outperforms baselines powered by the closed-source models GPT-4.1-mini and GPT-5-nano. Trajectory analyses further show that GraphDancer retrieves answer-bearing evidence more often and achieves higher accuracy when such evidence is found, while substantially reducing invalid-protocol outcomes.</p></details> | <details><summary>14 pa...</summary><p>14 pages. Code: https://github.com/leopoldwhite/GraphDancer. Project website: https://yuyangbai.com/graphdancer/</p></details> |
| **[DoubleFree:Exploiting Exploration History for Rollout-Free Multi-Task LLMs Training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34272v1)** | 2026-10-25 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multi-task post-training can develop domain specialists through independent reinforcement learning (RL) and consolidate their capabilities through multi-teacher on-policy distillation (MOPD). | **[Hi-Token: Hierarchical Coordinate Tokenization for Generative Visual Grounding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03471v3)** | 2026-10-25 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Generative Vision-Language Models (VLMs) express bounding boxes as coordinate sequences, whose token identities often leave axis and place value implicit. | **[Reinforcement Learning with Multi-Step Lookahead Information Via Adaptive Batching](https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.10418v2)** | 2026-10-25 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>We study tabular finite-horizon reinforcement learning problems with multiple steps of lookahead information. Before acting, the learner observes $\ell$ steps of future transition and reward realizations: the exact state the agent would reach and the rewards it would collect under any possible course of action. While it has been shown that such information can drastically boost the value, finding the optimal policy is NP-hard, and it is common to apply one of two tractable heuristics: processing the lookahead in chunks of predefined sizes ('fixed batching policies'), and model predictive control. We first illustrate the problems with these two approaches and propose utilizing the lookahead in adaptive (state-dependent) batches; we refer to such policies as adaptive batching policies (ABPs). We derive the optimal Bellman equations for these strategies and design an optimistic regret-minimizing algorithm that enables learning the optimal ABP when interacting with unknown environments. Our regret bounds are order-optimal up to a potential factor of the lookahead horizon $\ell$, which can usually be considered a small constant.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted to NeurIPS 2026</p></details> |
| **[CORAL: Learning Amyloid Fibril Ligand Docking with Cooperative Binding Rewards](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.17412v2)** | 2026-10-25 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>A hallmark of neurodegenerative diseases such as Alzheimer's and Parkinson's is the aberrant aggregation of proteins into amyloid fibrils, and small molecules that selectively bind to these fibrils hold promise as diagnostics, imaging probes, and therapeutics. Predicting how such ligands bind to fibril targets, however, presents two fundamental challenges. First, resolved co-crystal structures of amyloid-ligand complexes are exceptionally scarce; even with recent advances in cryo-EM only a handful have been structurally characterized, making supervised training of docking models impractical for this target class. Second, amyloid fibrils present a binding mode fundamentally different from globular proteins: ligands intercalate into longitudinal cross-$β$ grooves and stack cooperatively along the fibril axis, a geometry that existing docking models are not designed to capture. To address these challenges, we present CORAL (COopeRative Amyloid Ligand docking), a reinforcement learning framework that trains a generative docking model to produce ligand pose distributions tailored to the cross-$β$ groove geometry. Our reward explicitly incorporates cooperative ligand-ligand stacking energy alongside protein-ligand docking affinity, directly capturing the distinctive binding geometry of amyloid fibrils. We further introduce a curated evaluation set of amyloid-ligand complexes constructed from model-generated poses validated by domain experts. Experiments on both experimentally resolved structures and this evaluation set demonstrate improved pose quality and binding affinity correlation over existing docking baselines. Our project page is available at https://sunyasheng.github.io/projects/CORAL.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted at NeurIPS 2026. 27 pages. Project page: https://sunyasheng.github.io/projects/CORAL</p></details> |
| **[CORMA: Constraint-Routed Decision Mamba for Generalizable Multi-Constraint Offline Safe Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34208v1)** | 2026-10-25 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Offline safe reinforcement learning (RL) learns safety-aware policies from static datasets while satisfying safety constraints during deployment. Motivated by the strong trajectorymodeling capabilities of Transformers, recent Decision Transformer (DT)-based methods formulate offline safe RL as conditional sequence modeling by conditioning action generation on return-to-go (RTG) and cost-to-go (CTG) tokens, thereby enabling adaptation to varying safety budgets. However, existing DT-based methods are developed for single-constraint tasks. For tasks with multiple constraints, generalizing learned policies to unseen combinations of safety budgets remains challenging due to the conflicting requirements imposed by heterogeneous constraints. Moreover, the quadratic complexity of Transformer architectures significantly increases the computational overhead in multi-constraint tasks, as representing additional constraints requires more CTG tokens. Recently, Mamba has emerged as an efficient sequence-modeling backbone, offering linear computational complexity and strong generalization capabilities. In this paper, we propose CORMA, a constraint-routed decision Mamba framework for generalizable multi-constraint offline safe RL. CORMA decomposes policy inference into constraint-aware RTG estimation and adaptive action generation, where constraint-specific experts model feasible RTG distributions and a router-guided product-of-experts mechanism fuses these distributions to provide safety-aware guidance for a Mamba-based action decoder. A three-stage optimization strategy is designed to improve training stability. | **[A learner-free swarm reaches the explore-exploit equilibrium: A self-organized attractor approximating the reinforcement-learning solution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25113v3)** | 2026-10-25 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>The explore-exploit trade-off is the core problem of reinforcement learning (RL). In its multi-agent, resource-sharing form the optimum is not a scalar-return maximum but an equilibrium allocation -- the ideal-free distribution (IFD), the Nash equilibrium of a congestion game. We show that a minimal foraging swarm reaches the shape of this equilibrium as a self-organized attractor -- foragers in proportion to resource richness (matching slope 0.94) -- with no learner, no trained parameters, and no payoff or reward read anywhere in its dynamics. Agents explore by heavy-tailed (Levy) flights, recruit neighbours to a discovery through a threshold cascade on their proximity network, and disperse as a patch crowds; removing the coupling of recruitment to movement collapses the matching (slope 0.08). The reinforcement learning is at the level of the problem and the allocation reached, not the micro-rule. The swarm reaches this equilibrium rather than learning it, attaining its shape but not the exact equilibrium: it stays measurably exploitable (Nash-gap 0.58 versus ~0.01 for a converged learner). Against PPO learners (feedforward, centralized-critic, and graph message-passing) trained in the same sensing-limited environment, the reward-free swarm at zero training matches the IFD gradient more faithfully than any learner (slope ~1 vs 0.69-0.91) and ties the best learner's harvest where clustered (both plateau at ~0.80 of an oracle ceiling), while the learner collects more where resource is scarce (0.33 vs 0.22) and holds tighter per-capita intake. Learning thus buys precision and transfer, not a better allocation; neither message passing nor a centralized critic exceeds it at this budget. We read this not as beating RL, but as evidence that a minimal swarm's physics reaches the equilibrium RL seeks without a learner, a reward, or a trained parameter. A companion paper develops the mechanism.</p></details> | <details><summary>33 pa...</summary><p>33 pages, 12 figures, code and data: https://github.com/galenwilkerson/self-organizing-reinforcement-learning</p></details> |

Specifically, we prove that optimal planning with one-step look-ahead ($\ell=1$) can be solved in polynomial time through a novel linear programming formulation. In contrast, for $\ell \geq 2$, the problem becomes NP-hard. Our results delineate a precise boundary between tractable and intractable cases for the problem of planning with transition look-ahead in reinforcement learning.</p></details> |  |

| **[Reinforcement Learning for Adaptive Crowd Flow Control Using Dynamic Barriers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34163v1)** | 2026-10-25 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Crowd barriers are widely used to regulate pedestrian movement during large public events such as pilgrimages. Unlike supervised approaches the proposed framework allows the agent to explore barrier configurations that have never been observed in real data. We evaluate the learned policy under unseen numbers of pedestrians. The learned policy consistently outperforms the existing manually designed barrier configuration by reducing pedestrian stops while maintaining effective flow regulation. These results demonstrate that combining computer vision crowd simulation and reinforcement learning provides an effective framework for optimizing crowd control infrastructure.</p></details> | <details><summary>An ea...</summary><p>An earlier version was presented at the 26th Scientific Forum for Hajj, Umrah and Visit Research, Umm Al-Qura University (2026)</p></details> |
| **[Self-Evolution Reasoner: Continuous Optimization via Policy-Intrinsic Exploration and Exploitation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34137v1)** | 2026-10-25 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR) enables large language models (LLMs) to continuously self-improve their reasoning capabilities. Relying on external demonstrations can bypass this issue; however, the off-policy nature hinders genuine self-improvement and the mismatched-distribution reasoning can not be fully exploited. In this work, we propose Self-Evolution Policy Optimization (SEPO), a framework that drives continuous reasoning capability evolution via guided exploration and in-distribution exploitation. Specifically, SEPO utilizes labels to elicit correct reasoning from the policy, unlocking the exploration potential. By treating current policy conditioned on this in-distribution reasoning as a self-teacher, SEPO distills the newly discovered solution back into the policy. Through this iterative cycle of policy-intrinsic exploration and exploitation, SEPO achieves robust and continuous capability evolution. Across scientific, tool-use, and complex logical reasoning domains, SEPO consistently achieves the best performance under identical training time or steps, demonstrating superior efficiency and convergence ceilings. | **[Harness Scaling for Generalizable Agentic Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29458v2)** | 2026-10-25 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Agent harnesses have become a central component of modern agentic systems, shaping how models use tools, manage context, and coordinate long-horizon interaction. Realizing the benefits of these harnesses depends on how effectively the underlying model can make use of them, motivating reinforcement learning directly through the harness. While recent efforts have explored harness diversification to reduce specialization to particular execution settings, how to systematically construct and organize harness diversity for cross-harness generalization remains insufficiently understood. This work studies Harness Scaling as a distinct scaling dimension for agentic RL, focusing on structured variation in how policies execute and interact with the same underlying task and environment. Concretely, we construct a factorized harness space, select a compact training subset with balanced factorial coverage, and organize cross-harness training through locality-aware task assignment. Across multiple benchmarks, model scales, and RL algorithms, Harness Scaling consistently improves generalization to unseen harness combinations. Moreover, policies trained through structured white-box harness variation transfer effectively to unseen external black-box harnesses, outperforming policies trained through a single complex black-box harness. These results establish harness diversity as a meaningful scaling dimension for agentic RL and highlight the importance of how this diversity is organized for cross-harness generalization.</p></details> |  |

A behavioral verifier compares executed trajectories with shared-policy recommendations; its flags drive a token mechanism combining slashing, burning, rewards, and compensation. We derive a one-round incentive condition that accounts for action-dependent compensation and the payoff from deviation, and a finite expected exclusion-time bound under explicit conditional detection and accounting assumptions, showing perfect detection is unnecessary. Together, these results characterize how behavioral verification and token feedback can support accountable cooperative learning.</p></details> | <details><summary>12 pa...</summary><p>12 pages, 4 figures, 6 tables. Submitted to the IEEE Internet of Things Journal</p></details> |

| **[EmbodiedWorld-200K: Toward Embodied World Agents via Embodied-Planning Dataset and Interactive World Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25959v2)** | 2026-10-25 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Building embodied agents that can perceive, reason, and act across diverse 3D worlds is a central research direction toward artificial general intelligence. Yet most existing work remains confined to specific tasks in a single scene and transfers poorly to new environments, leaving general embodied agents for open-world settings under-explored. We refer to such agents as Embodied World Agents (EWAs), whose defining property is the ability to understand diverse visual environments and execute general actions in open-world conditions. To advance EWA research, we study open-world embodied navigation-planning and release EmbodiedWorld-200K, a large-scale dataset of over 200K samples spanning diverse scenes. Each example is equipped with multi-level annotations, ranging from low-level camera-motion trajectories to high-level semantic instructions. The dataset also provides unified evaluation splits and task-specific metrics, supporting offline pretraining and fair evaluation of EWAs. Beyond offline supervision, a defining characteristic of an EWA is its interaction with the environment; training solely on offline data is therefore insufficient, especially for generalizing to scenes not covered in the training distribution. Building dedicated real interactive environments, however, is often prohibitively expensive. To address this, we propose a novel training scheme that employs a video world model as a low-cost environment proxy and performs interactive reinforcement-learning (RL) post-training of the EWA within it. Empirically, pretraining on EmbodiedWorld-200K substantially improves the open-world embodied planning ability of the EWA, and further RL post-training inside a world model yields clear gains on out-of-distribution scenes. The dataset, baseline checkpoints, and training and evaluation code are available at https://xiaokunfeng.github.io/EmbodiedWorld-200K/.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted by NeurIPS 2026~</p></details> |
| **[Terminus-4B: Can a Smaller Model Replace Frontier LLMs at Agentic Execution Tasks?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.03195v3)** | 2026-10-25 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Modern coding agents increasingly delegate specialized subtasks to subagents, which are smaller, focused agentic loops that handle narrow responsibilities like search, debugging or terminal execution. This architectural pattern keeps the main agent's context window clean by isolating verbose outputs (e.g. build logs, test results, etc.) within the subagent context. Typically when agents employ subagents for such tasks, they use frontier models as these subagents. In this paper, we investigate whether a finetuned small language model (SLM) can achieve comparable performance to frontier models in the task of agentic terminal execution. We present Terminus-4B, which is a post-trained Qwen3-4B model via Supervised Finetuning (SFT) and Reinforcement Learning (RL) using rubric-based LLM-as-judge reward, specifically for this task. In our extensive evaluation spanning various frontier models, training ablations and main agent configurations, we find that Terminus-4B is able to reduce the token usage of the main agent by up to ~30% compared to the No Subagent baseline with no impact to agent performance on benchmarks like SWE-Bench Pro and our internal SWE-Bench C# benchmark, which tends to be heavy in verbose execution tasks. Furthermore, Terminus-4B improves key metrics showing the main agent relying on the outputs of the subagent and doing fewer terminal execution tasks by itself. We see that our model not only closes the gap between the Vanilla Qwen model and frontier models like Claude Sonnet / Opus / GPT-5.3-Codex, but often even exceeds their performance.</p></details> | <details><summary>The c...</summary><p>The current article involves some product IP issues and needs to be withdrawn and re-approved</p></details> |
| **[Leveraging Dale's Principle as an Inductive Bias in Recurrent Neural Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34052v1)** | 2026-10-25 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>One of the fundamental differences between biological neural networks and artificial neural networks (ANNs) is that biological neurons obey Dale's principle: each neuron projects either exclusively excitatory or exclusively inhibitory outputs. While this constraint is central to biological circuits, previous attempts to impose Dale's principle in ANNs have often led to training instability or degraded performance. In this work, we introduce EISep RNN, a recurrent neural network architecture that implements Dale's principle through a learnable excitatory-inhibitory separation. Across a series of learning settings, including multi-task learning, continual learning, and reinforcement learning, EISep achieves more stable training dynamics, competitive or improved performance, and sparser, more modular connectivity than vanilla RNNs and previous Dale-constrained models. EISep also exhibits stable and informative latent dynamics. These results establish Dale's principle as a biologically grounded and computationally effective inductive bias for recurrent neural networks.</p></details> | 48 pages, 38 figures |
| **[Minimax Regret for Sobolev Kernel-Based Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34045v1)** | 2026-10-25 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>We study the minimax regret rate for episodic kernel-based reinforcement learning, where the expected reward and transition density belong to a Sobolev reproducing kernel Hilbert space, with continuous state and action spaces, and adversarial initial states. For Sobolev smoothness parameter $ν>0$, state dimension $d_S$, and action dimension $d_A$, we prove that the minimax expected regret over $T$ episodes is $\widetildeΘ(T^{\max\{e_A,e_B\}})$, where $e_A=\frac{ν+d_A}{2ν+d_A}$, $e_B=\frac{ν+d_A+3d_S/2}{2ν+d_A+2d_S}$ with the other model parameters fixed. The two exponents reflect the distinct difficulties of action selection and learning across states. We introduce Sobolev Optimal Value Iteration (SOVI), which attains this rate from observed rewards and transitions. For fixed smoothness and reference-kernel lengthscale, its regret bound has a prefactor polynomial in the state-action dimension $d=d_S+d_A$, horizon $H$, transition and reward RKHS norm bounds $u,B_r$, and kernel norm-comparison factor $Λ$, up to logarithmic factors. The algorithm separates initial action selection from continuation-value estimation and combines local polynomial approximation with the shared RKHS structure of the approximation errors. Our matching upper and lower bounds determine the optimal dependence on the number of episodes for Sobolev kernel-based reinforcement learning. Thus, we partially address the open problem in Vakili(2024) under the condition of the Sobolev kernel setup.</p></details> |  |

## Robotics

| **[Nonprehensile Robotic Catching Using Virtual Constraints](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35851v1)** | 2026-10-26 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>The problem of nonprehensile robotic catching is addressed using virtual nonholonomic constraints (VNHCs); the motion of the robot end-effector is designed and controlled directly in terms of the states of the object before it is caught on the surface of the end-effector. In contrast to contemporary approaches, our approach avoids the need for object trajectory prediction, and consequent time-varying robot trajectory generation. With a focus on the planar case, we demonstrate that our VNHC approach can be used to catch an object on an end-effector at a desired height, with minimal relative normal velocity at the contact instant. Moreover, the VNHC function depends only on the desired catching height, and the same VNHC can be used for a range of initial conditions. We use model predictive control (MPC) to bring the object and end-effector to rest at a desired location after contact is made. The approach is validated in simulation.</p></details> | <details><summary>9 pag...</summary><p>9 pages, 6 figures; an abridged version of this article has been submitted to the 2027 American Control Conference (ACC)</p></details> |
| **[Pretraining Knows What, Simulation Teaches How: Compositional Generalization in a Sim-Trained Robot Agent](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35817v1)** | 2026-10-26 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>A robot policy needs to understand what a task is asking for in a scene, and how to move the robot to accomplish that task. To achieve these two skills, we introduce Zero-real Agent Policy (ZAP), a recipe that teaches a small off-the-shelf vision-language model to control a robot via a simple tool harness. We use a pretrained 27B parameter model to bootstrap world understanding and reasoning, followed by supervised fine-tuning and GRPO on simple simulated tasks to teach it how to move the robot. Without any real-world robot data during training, ZAP reaches 96.7% success on real-world tasks, matching GPT-6 Astra in our harness and outperforming the MolmoAct2 VLA (33.3%), while running 3x faster and 17x cheaper than GPT-6 Astra. We demonstrate that ZAP also generalizes to completely new objects, goals, strategies, and language by seating a test tube in a rack (and choosing a different hole in the rack when prompted), handing a test tube between arms mid-air, drawing an X on a tic-tac-toe board, and playing chess. These goals and types of manipulation don't appear in our training data. Furthermore, ZAP also transfers to an unseen embodiment. On a single-arm Franka, ZAP reaches 55.9% on RoboLab-120, 4.9 percentage points ahead of the leading VLA/WAM baseline.</p></details> |  |
| **[SPECOLA: A Tool for Specification Driven Robot Evaluation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35730v1)** | 2026-10-26 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Simulation benchmarks are a useful way to measure progress in robotics; typically they provide success rate as the dominant measure of performance. However, success rate reports only whether a rollout completed the task, hiding by what margin it succeeded, how sensitive the success is to goal parameter changes, requirements it may have violated, and whether success persisted. Signal Temporal Logic (STL) provides a formal language for capturing specifications; it can be used to define requirements, and measure how close a robot behavior is to satisfying or violating them. Using STL monitors in a simulator, however, requires exposing simulator state and mapping it to specification-relevant signals. We present Specola, a tool for evaluating robot behaviors in simulation with respect to STL specifications. Specola provides an interface for writing specifications over simulator signals, and creates and manages the relevant monitors while a policy executes. We provide a Python API and command-line interface for integrating monitoring into existing policy execution loops. We provide an adapter interface for connecting new simulators, and provide adapters for nine simulation environments and benchmarks, including LIBERO, RoboCasa, Isaac Lab, and RoboDojo. We provide case studies that illustrate the nuanced evaluation that Specola enables. Code and adapters are available at https://github.com/specola-eval/specola.</p></details> |  |
| **[TrySee: Modular Perception-Enhanced VLM Grounding for Closed-Loop Robot Task Planning in Specialized Domains](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35687v1)** | 2026-10-26 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Vision Language Models (VLMs) have shown impressive capabilities in high-level reasoning and task planning for robots. However, specialized domains remain a challenge for VLMs' image interpretation and grounding abilities. We introduce TrySee, a modular closed-loop robot task planning pipeline integrating general-purpose VLM reasoning with domain-specific perception and established robotics components. The system consists of an inherently reactive cyclic planning pipeline, augmented with domain-specific modular vision preprocessing, to provide online grounding. Finally, a dynamic preprocessing selection mechanism contributes to the scalability of the pipeline. The system requires neither VLM fine-tuning nor end-to-end demonstrations and adapts through replaceable perception modules and motion planning. The approach is evaluated on planning tasks across 5 widely different domains, including a real-world robot disassembly task. The field tests in a brownfield environment demonstrate the approach's practicality in integrating VLM planning into specialized robotic systems with limited domain-specific training data.</p></details> | <details><summary>To be...</summary><p>To be published in the proceedings of the 2026 IEEE International Conference on Robotics and Biomimetics (ROBIO)</p></details> |
| **[FISRF: Flexible Instructional Scaffolding Reflection Framework for Robotics Task Planning with Large Language Model](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35686v1)** | 2026-10-26 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large Language Models (LLMs) are widely adopted for reflective error correction in task planning; however, existing approaches primarily rely on autonomous exploration, rendering them prone to accumulating erroneous experiences during early interactions with new environments. Experiments in ALFWorld and on real-world robotic platforms demonstrate that FISRF reduces the average number of error-correction rounds by 37% and redundant reasoning by 25.1%, while improving the overall task success rate by 8%. Project webpage : https://fisrfplanning.github.io/</p></details> |  |
| **[From Language to Drives: A Multimodal Framework for Aligning Autonomous Robots with Human Purposes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35629v1)** | 2026-10-26 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>This work explores a multimodal approach to the alignment of the behavior of open-ended learning autonomous robots with human purposes through the use of large language models as cultural enablers. | **[Robot-Aligned 3D Gaussian Splatting for Precise Reaching with Wrist-Camera VLA Policies](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35626v1)** | 2026-10-26 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Efficient robotic data collection remains a major bottleneck for vision-language-action (VLA) policy learning, especially for tasks requiring accurate end-effector positioning and camera-action alignment. With $π$0.5, 1,500 generated trajectories achieve 80.0\% precise-reaching success across three targets, compared with 58.7\% using 150 teleoperated demonstrations, while requiring less than 10 minutes of active human capture. | **[RoboDoctor: Training-Free Recursive Self-Improvement for Robot Policies](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35604v1)** | 2026-10-26 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Generalist robot policies and language-model agents provide complementary capabilities, but completing a task also requires adapting their use to the consequences of physical interaction. | **[Distributed Predictive Flocking for a Swarm of Miniature Vibration-Driven Robots in a Confined Arena](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35582v1)** | 2026-10-26 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Coordinating many miniature robots in a confined arena, with no leader, central planner, or global map, is a core challenge for swarm robotics. | **[AffordSim: A Scalable Data Generator and Benchmark for Affordance-Aware Robotic Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.11674v3)** | 2026-10-26 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Many everyday robot manipulation skills are affordance-dependent, with success determined by whether the robot contacts the functional object region required by the subsequent action. | **[CognitiveReality: Robot-Agnostic Semantic Gaussian Mapping with an LLM Agent for Immersive Collaborative VR Teleoperation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.31418v2)** | 2026-10-26 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>A photorealistic 3D view tells a teleoperator where a robot is, but not what the scene contains, how well each object has been observed, or how to turn pointing and speech into robot action. | **[Labference: Benchmarking the Reasoning-Action Connection in Robotic Laboratories](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35252v1)** | 2026-10-26 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Realizing Embodied AI for Science requires robots to translate experimental goals into reliable physical procedures, reducing hazardous and repetitive human work while generating evidence for scientific discovery. | **[Probing Collision Grounding in Vision-Language Models for Safe Human-Robot Collaboration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.31196v2)** | 2026-10-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Safe human--robot collaboration requires more than visual description: a monitor must determine whether the robot body is safely separated, already colliding with the scene or a person, or about to collide. Built in Habitat~3.0, TouchSafeBench contains 2,940 simulated indoor co-presence episodes across social navigation and social rearrangement, with synchronized multi-view RGB-D observations, top-down trajectory maps, calibrated camera metadata, and simulator-derived contact labels. Across three frontier or robotics-oriented VLMs and nine visual representations, current models remain far from reliable: the best average Macro-F1 stays below 50\%, explicit depth is not automatically transformed into robot-body collision evidence, and robot--scene contact is consistently harder than human-contact risk. | **[VORL-EXPLORE: A Hybrid Learning Planning Approach to Multi-Robot Exploration in Dynamic Environments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.07973v2)** | 2026-10-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Hierarchical multi-robot exploration decouples frontier allocation from local navigation, making systems brittle in dynamic environments. Updated manuscript</p></details> |
| **[CARE: Consistent Action-Response Execution for Human-Guided Robot Transport](https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.16686v2)** | 2026-10-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Human-guided collaborative transport becomes interaction-critical when the operator can continue along a route segment that is not executable by the robot--payload system. We formulate local control over executable-action-indexed joint human--robot transitions and instantiate it in Consistent Action--Response Execution (CARE). Across training and held-out maps, CARE preserves near-saturated performance when the indicated route remains locally usable and achieves the highest observed success and service availability when the operator can continue but the robot must detour. Substantially revised manuscript with a new title and updated methods and experiments</p></details> |

We evaluate the tracking success of the generated transitions on Unitree~G1 and~ROBOTIS K1. | **[LogLook: Execution-Grounded Memory for Frontier-Model Robot Planners](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32348v1)** | 2026-10-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Many manipulation tasks require a robot to remember what it has done and what it has seen. | **[DynaSlots: Slot-Centric 3D Dynamics Pretraining for Robot Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32330v1)** | 2026-10-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Robot manipulation involves localized changes: picking up a cup moves the cup while leaving the surrounding scene largely unchanged. | **[RMMBench: A Comprehensive Benchmark for Robotic Mobile Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.05414v2)** | 2026-10-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Although the advancement of vision-language models (VLMs) has endowed robots with enhanced environmental understanding and task reasoning, a comprehensive evaluation methodology is important to advance the integration of VLMs in robotic navigation and manipulation. | **[MAST-VLA: Skill-Conditioned Adaptation with Trajectory-Affordance Priors for Multi-Skill Robot Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32312v1)** | 2026-10-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Generalist robot manipulation requires a single vision-language-action (VLA) policy to execute diverse skills across scenes and instructions. | **[3D Generation for Embodied AI and Robotic Simulation: A Survey](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.26509v4)** | 2026-10-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Embodied AI and robotic systems increasingly depend on scalable, diverse, and physically grounded 3D content for simulation-based training and real-world deployment. This survey reviews 3D generation for embodied AI and organizes the literature around three roles that 3D generation plays in embodied systems. In Data Generator, 3D generation produces simulation-ready objects and assets, including articulated, physically grounded, and deformable content for downstream interaction; in Simulation Environments, it constructs interactive and task-oriented worlds, spanning structure-aware, controllable, and agentic scene generation; and in Sim2Real Bridge, it supports digital twin reconstruction, data augmentation, and synthetic demonstrations for downstream robot learning and real-world transfer. We also show that the field is shifting from visual realism toward interaction readiness, and we identify the main bottlenecks, including limited physical annotations, the gap between geometric quality and physical validity, fragmented evaluation, and the persistent sim-to-real divide, that must be addressed for 3D generation to become a dependable foundation for embodied intelligence. | **[RGraph-Eval: Task-Grounded Scene Graphs for Evaluating Robot Video Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31121v2)** | 2026-10-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Generated robot videos can look convincing while depicting the wrong interaction: a different object is moved, a grasp disappears, or a placement ends outside its destination. | **[From Tabletop to Walking Bot: Deploying a VLA Model on a Legged Manipulator Robot](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32271v1)** | 2026-10-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Vision-language-action (VLA) models are typically pretrained on broad, diverse datasets and subsequently fine-tuned on a comparatively small dataset tailored to a specific target robot. | **[DASH: A da Vinci Adapter for Serial-link and Humanoid Robots as an Accessible Platform for Surgical Robotics Research](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.05792v2)** | 2026-10-22 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Robotic minimally invasive surgery offers well-documented clinical benefits, but the cost and infrastructure requirements of purpose-built platforms limit access in rural and lower-resourced facilities.
